|first1=Cornelius J. |last2=Klehe |first2=Ute-Christine |last3=Berchtold |first3=Matthias |last4=Kleinmann |first4=Martin |title=Reasons for Being Selective When Choosing Personnel Selection Procedures |journal=International Journal of Selection and Assessment |date=March 2010 |volume=18 |issue=1 |pages=17–27 |doi=10.1111/j.1468-2389.2010.00485.x |s2cid=145784621 |url=https://www.zora.uzh.ch/id/eprint/34816/1/klehe.pdf |access-date=2020-09-01 |archive-date=2021-05-03 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20210503092124/https://www.zora.uzh.ch/id/eprint/34816/1/klehe.pdf }}</ref> Though the applicant's perception of the interview process may not influence the interviewer(s) ability to distinguish between individuals' suitability, applicants reactions are important as those who react negatively to the selection process are more likely to withdraw from the selection process.<ref name="Hausknecht">{{cite journal |last1=Hausknecht |first1=John P. |last2=Day |first2=David V. |last3=Thomas |first3=Scott C. |title=Applicant Reactions to Selection Procedures: An Updated Model and Meta-Analysis |journal=Personnel Psychology |date=September 2004 |volume=57 |issue=3 |pages=639–683 |doi=10.1111/j.1744-6570.2004.00003.x |hdl=1813/75281 |url=https://digitalcommons.ilr.cornell.edu/cgi/viewcontent.cgi?article=1126&context=articles |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20091217173627/http://digitalcommons.ilr.cornell.edu/cgi/viewcontent.cgi?article=1126&context=articles |url-status=dead |archive-date=December 17, 2009 |hdl-access=free }}</ref><ref name="Truxillo">{{cite journal |last1=Truxillo |first1=Donald M. |last2=Bauer |first2=Talya N. |last3=Campion |first3=Michael A. |last4=Paronto |first4=Matthew E. |title=Selection fairness information and applicant reactions: A longitudinal field study. |url=https://archive.org/details/sim_journal-of-applied-psychology_2002-12_87_6/page/1020 |journal=Journal of Applied Psychology |date=2002 |volume=87 |issue=6 |pages=1020–1031 |doi=10.1037/0021-9010.87.6.1020 |pmid=12558210 }}</ref><ref name="Chapman7">{{cite journal |last1=Chapman |first1=Derek S. |last2=Zweig |first2=David I. |title=Developing a nomological network for interview structure: Antecedents and consequences of the structured selection interview |journal=Personnel Psychology |date=September 2005 |volume=58 |issue=3 |pages=673–702 |doi=10.1111/j.1744-6570.2005.00516.x }}</ref> They are less likely to accept a job offer, apply on future occasions,<ref>{{cite book |last1=Gilliland |first1=Stephen W. |last2=Steiner |first2=Dirk D. |chapter=Causes and Consequences of Applicant Perceptions of Unfairness |pages=175–195 |chapter-url=https://books.google.com/books?id=CbkG1IvGOkYC&pg=PA175 |editor1-last=Cropanzano |editor1-first=Russell |title=Justice in the Workplace: From Theory to Practice |year=2001 |publisher=Psychology Press |isbn=978-0-8058-2694-4 }}</ref> or to speak highly of the organization to others and to be a customer of that business.<ref name="Hausknecht" /><ref name="Truxillo" /><ref>{{cite journal |last1=Ambrose |first1=Maureen L. |last2=Cropanzano |first2=Russell |title=A longitudinal analysis of organizational fairness: An examination of reactions to tenure and promotion decisions. |url=https://archive.org/details/sim_journal-of-applied-psychology_2003-04_88_2/page/266 |journal=Journal of Applied Psychology |date=2003 |volume=88 |issue=2 |pages=266–275 |doi=10.1037/0021-9010.88.2.266 |pmid=12731710 }}</ref> Compared to other selection methods, such as personality or cognitive ability tests, applicants, from different cultures may have positive opinions about interviews.<ref name="Hausknecht" /><ref>{{cite journal |last1=Anderson |first1=Neil |last2=Salgado |first2=Jesús F. |last3=Hülsheger |first3=Ute R. |title=Applicant Reactions in Selection: Comprehensive meta-analysis into reaction generalization versus situational specificity: Applicant Reactions Meta-Analysis |journal=International Journal of Selection and Assessment |date=16 August 2010 |volume=18 |issue=3 |pages=291–304 |doi=10.1111/j.1468-2389.2010.00512.x |s2cid=146666324 |url=https://cris.maastrichtuniversity.nl/en/publications/62a25a2a-f99b-487f-8d7b-9fc06e06249c }}</ref>

====Interview design====
Interview design can influence applicants' positive and negative reactions, though research findings on applicants preferences for structured compared to unstructured interviews appear contradictory.<ref name="Chapman" /><ref>{{cite journal |last1=Chapman |first1=Derek S. |last2=Rowe |first2=Patricia M. |title=The Influence of Videoconference Technology and Interview Structure on the Recruiting Function of the Employment Interview: A Field Experiment |journal=International Journal of Selection and Assessment |date=September 2002 |volume=10 |issue=3 |pages=185–197 |doi=10.1111/1468-2389.00208 }}</ref> Applicants' negative reactions to structured interviews may be reduced by providing information about the job and organization.<ref>Dipboye et al., 1998</ref> Providing interview questions to applicants before the interview, or telling them how their answers will be evaluated, are also received positively.<ref>{{cite journal |last1=Day |first1=Arla L. |last2=Carroll |first2=Sarah A. |title=Situational and Patterned Behavior Description Interviews: A Comparison of Their Validity, Correlates, and Perceived Fairness |journal=Human Performance |date=January 2003 |volume=16 |issue=1 |pages=25–47 |doi=10.1207/s15327043hup1601_2 |s2cid=144419119 }}</ref>

====Types of questions====
The type of questions asked can affect applicant reactions. General questions are viewed more positively than situational or behavioral questions<ref>{{cite journal |last1=Conway |first1=James M. |last2=Peneno |first2=Gina M. |title=Comparing structured interview question types: Construct validity and applicant reactions |journal=Journal of Business and Psychology |date=1999 |volume=13 |issue=4 |pages=485–506 |doi=10.1023/a:1022914803347 |s2cid=141482090 }}</ref> and 'puzzle' interview questions may be perceived as negative being perceived unrelated to the job, unfair, or unclear how to answer.<ref>{{cite journal |last1=Wright |first1=Chris W. |last2=Sablynski |first2=Chris J. |last3=Manson |first3=Todd M. |last4=Oshiro |first4=Steven |title=Why Are Manhole Covers Round? A Laboratory Study of Reactions to Puzzle Interviews: Reactions to Puzzle Interviews |journal=Journal of Applied Social Psychology |date=November 2012 |volume=42 |issue=11 |pages=2834–2857 |doi=10.1111/j.1559-1816.2012.00963.x }}</ref> Using questions that discriminate unfairly in law unsurprisingly are viewed negatively with applicants less likely to accept a job offer, or to recommend the organization to others.<ref name="legal1"/>

Some of the questions and concerns on the mind of the hiring manager include:

* Does this person have the skills I need to get the job done?
* Will they fit in with the department or team?
* Can I manage this person?
* Does this person demonstrate honesty, integrity, and a good work ethic?
* What motivates this person?
* Do I like this person, and do they get along with others?
* Will they focus on tasks and stick to the job until it is done?
* Will this person perform up to the level the company requires for success?

A sample of intention behind questions asked for understanding observable responses, displayed character, and underlying motivation:

* What did the candidate really do in this job?
* What role did they play, supportive or leading?
* How much influence did the candidate exert on the outcomes of projects?
* How did the candidate handle problems that came up?
* How does this candidate come across?
* How serious is the candidate about their career and this job?
* Are they bright and likable?
* Did the candidate prepare for this interview?
* Is the candidate being forthright with information?
* Does this person communicate well in a somewhat stressful face-to-face conversation? 
* Does the candidate stay focused on the question asked or ramble along?
* Did the candidate exhibit good judgment in the career moves he or she made?
* Did the candidate grow in their job and take on more responsibilities over time or merely do the same thing repeatedly?
* Did the candidate demonstrate leadership, integrity, effective communications, teamwork, and persuasion skills (among others)?

====Additional factors====
The 'friendliness' of the interviewer may be equated to fairness of the process and improve the likelihood of accepting a job offer,<ref>{{cite journal |last1=Chapman |first1=Derek |last2=Webster |first2=Jane |title=Toward an integrated model of applicant reactions and job choice |journal=The International Journal of Human Resource Management |date=June 2006 |volume=17 |issue=6 |pages=1032–1057 |doi=10.1080/09585190600696572 |s2cid=146463390 }}</ref> and face-to-face interviews compared to video conferencing and telephone interviews.<ref name="Chapman2"/> In video conferencing interviews the perception of the interviewer may be viewed as less personable, trustworthy, and competent.<ref>{{cite journal |last1=J. Sears |first1=Greg |last2=Zhang |first2=Haiyan |last3=H. Wiesner |first3=Willi |last4=D. Hackett |first4=Rick |last5=Yuan |first5=Yufei |title=A comparative assessment of videoconference and face-to-face employment interviews |journal=Management Decision |date=2 September 2013 |volume=51 |issue=8 |pages=1733–1752 |doi=10.1108/MD-09-2012-0642 }}</ref>

===Interview anxiety===
Interview anxiety refers to having unpleasant feelings before or during a job interview.<ref name="McCarthy, J. 2004">{{cite journal |last1=McCarthy |first1=Julie |last2=Goffin |first2=Richard |title=Measuring Job Interview Anxiety: Beyond Weak Knees and Sweaty Palms |journal=Personnel Psychology |date=September 2004 |volume=57 |issue=3 |pages=607–637 |doi=10.1111/j.1744-6570.2004.00002.x |s2cid=70954 }}</ref> It also reflects the fear of partaking in an interview.<ref name="The effect of coaching interviewees"/> Job candidates may feel this increased sense of anxiety because they have little to no control over the interview process.<ref>{{cite journal |last1=Jones |first1=David B. |last2=Pinkney |first2=James W. |year=1989 |title=An Exploratory Assessment of the Sources of Job-Interviewing Anxiety in College Students |url=https://archive.org/details/sim_journal-of-college-student-development_1989-11_30_6/page/553 |journal=Journal of College Student Development |volume=30 |issue=6 |pages=553–60 }}</ref> It could also be because they have to speak with a stranger.<ref>{{cite journal |last1=Ayres |first1=Joe |last2=Keereetaweep |first2=Tanichya |last3=Chen |first3=Pao-En |last4=Edwards |first4=Patricia A. |title=Communication apprehension and employment interviews |journal=Communication Education |date=January 1998 |volume=47 |issue=1 |pages=1–17 |doi=10.1080/03634529809379106 }}</ref> Due to this fear, anxious candidates display certain behaviors or traits that signal to the interviewer that they are anxious. Examples of such behaviors include frequent pauses, speaking more slowly than usual, and biting or licking of lips.<ref name="Feiler, A. 2016">{{cite journal |last1=Feiler |first1=Amanda R. |last2=Powell |first2=Deborah M. |title=Behavioral Expression of Job Interview Anxiety |journal=Journal of Business and Psychology |date=March 2016 |volume=31 |issue=1 |pages=155–171 |doi=10.1007/s10869-015-9403-z |s2cid=144327151 }}</ref>

Research has identified five dimensions of interview anxiety: communication anxiety, social anxiety, performance anxiety, behavioral anxiety and appearance anxiety.<ref name="McCarthy, J. 2004"/> Further research shows that both the interviewer and applicant agree that speaking slowly is a clear sign of interview anxiety. However, they do not agree on other anxiety indicators such as frequent pauses and biting or licking of lips.<ref name="Feiler, A. 2016" /> Trait judgments are also related to interview anxiety and can affect interviewer perceptions of anxiety. Low assertiveness has been identified as the key trait related to interview anxiety. Thus, the most important indicators of interview anxiety are slow speech rate and low assertiveness.<ref name="Feiler, A. 2016" />

Another issue in interview anxiety is gender differences. Although females report being more anxious than males in interviews, their anxiety is not as readily detected as that for males. This can be explained by the Sex-Linked Anxiety Coping Theory (SCT). This theory suggests that females cope better than males when they are anxious in interviews.<ref>{{cite journal |last1=Feeney |first1=Justin R. |last2=McCarthy |first2=Julie M. |last3=Goffin |first3=Richard |title=Applicant Anxiety: Examining the sex-linked anxiety coping theory in job interview contexts: Interview Anxiety and Sex-linked Coping |journal=International Journal of Selection and Assessment |date=September 2015 |volume=23 |issue=3 |pages=295–305 |doi=10.1111/ijsa.12115 |s2cid=4498027 }}</ref>

====Implications for applicants====
Whether anxieties come from individual differences or from the interview setting, they have important costs for job candidates. These include: limiting effective communication and display of future potential,<ref>{{cite journal |last1=Posner |first1=Barry Z. |title=Comparing recruiter, student, and faculty perceptions of important applicant and job characteristics |journal=Personnel Psychology |date=June 1981 |volume=34 |issue=2 |pages=329–339 |doi=10.1111/j.1744-6570.1981.tb00946.x }}</ref> reducing interview performance and evaluation despite potential fit for the job,<ref name="McCarthy, J. 2004"/> and reducing the chance of a second interview compared to less anxious individuals.<ref>{{cite journal |last1=Cook |first1=Kevin W. |last2=Vance |first2=Carol A. |last3=Spector |first3=Paul E. |title=The Relation of Candidate Personality With Selection-Interview Outcomes |journal=Journal of Applied Social Psychology |date=April 2000 |volume=30 |issue=4 |pages=867–885 |doi=10.1111/j.1559-1816.2000.tb02828.x |s2cid=145369866 }}</ref>
Speaking slowly and low assertiveness have the strongest negative impact on perceptions of interview anxiety. Thus, candidates who experience anxiety in interviews should try to display assertive behaviors such as being dominant, professional, optimistic, attentive and confident<ref name="Feiler, A. 2016"/> In addition, they should speak at a consistent pace that is not unusually slow.

====Implications for organizations====
Applicants who view the selection process more favorably tend to be more positive about the organization, and are likely to influence an organization's reputation.<ref name="McCarthy, J. 2004"/><ref>{{cite journal |last1=Macan |first1=Therese Hoff |last2=Avedon |first2=Marcia J. |last3=Paese |first3=Matthew |last4=Smith |first4=David E. |title=The effects of applicants' reactions to cognitive ability tests and an assessment center |journal=Personnel Psychology |date=December 1994 |volume=47 |issue=4 |pages=715–738 |doi=10.1111/j.1744-6570.1994.tb01573.x }}</ref> whereas, in contrast, anxious or uncomfortable during their interview may view an organization less favorably, causing the otherwise qualified candidates not accepting a job offer.<ref name="McCarthy, J. 2004"/> If an applicant is nervous, they might not act the same way they would on the job, making it harder for organizations to use the interview for predicting someone's future job performance.<ref name="McCarthy, J. 2004"/>

==Legal issues ==
In many countries laws are put into place to prevent organizations from engaging in discriminatory practices against protected classes when selecting individuals for jobs.<ref>This is not meant to be a complete explanation of employment law or should it be construed as legal advice.  This merely attempts to explain certain laws that are applicable to the employment interview. Please seek legal counsel before taking action based on the content of this information.</ref> In the United States, it is unlawful for private employers with 15 or more employees along with state and local government employers to discriminate against applicants based on the following: race, color, sex (including pregnancy), national origin, age (40 or over), disability, or genetic information (note: additional classes may be protected depending on state or local law). More specifically, an employer cannot legally "fail or refuse to hire or to discharge any individual, or otherwise discriminate against any individual with respect to his compensation, terms, conditions, or privilege of employment" or "to limit, segregate, or classify his employees or applicants for employment in any way which would deprive or tend to deprive any individual of employment opportunities or otherwise adversely affect his status as an employee."<ref>{{cite journal |last1=Myors |first1=Brett |last2=Lievens |first2=Filip |last3=Schollaert |first3=Eveline |last4=Van Hoye |first4=Greet |last5=Cronshaw |first5=Steven F. |last6=Mladinic |first6=Antonio |last7=Rodríguez |first7=Viviana |last8=Aguinis |first8=Herman |last9=Steiner |first9=Dirk D. |last10=Rolland |first10=Florence |last11=Schuler |first11=Heinz |last12=Frintrup |first12=Andreas |last13=Nikolaou |first13=Ioannis |last14=Tomprou |first14=Maria |last15=Subramony |first15=S. |last16=Raj |first16=Shabu B. |last17=Tzafrir |first17=Shay |last18=Bamberger |first18=Peter |last19=Bertolino |first19=Marilena |last20=Mariani |first20=Marco |last21=Fraccaroli |first21=Franco |last22=Sekiguchi |first22=Tomoki |last23=Onyura |first23=Betty |last24=Yang |first24=Hyuckseung |last25=Anderson |first25=Neil |last26=Evers |first26=Arne |last27=Chernyshenko |first27=Oleksandr |last28=Englert |first28=Paul |last29=Kriek |first29=Hennie J. |last30=Joubert |first30=Tina |last31=Salgado |first31=Jesús F. |last32=König |first32=Cornelius J. |last33=Thommen |first33=Larissa A. |last34=Chuang |first34=Aichia |last35=Sinangil |first35=Handan Kepir |last36=Bayazit |first36=Mahmut |last37=Cook |first37=Mark |last38=Shen |first38=Winny |last39=Sackett |first39=Paul R. |title=International Perspectives on the Legal Environment for Selection |journal=Industrial and Organizational Psychology |date=June 2008 |volume=1 |issue=2 |pages=206–246 |doi=10.1111/j.1754-9434.2008.00040.x |hdl=11424/241303 |s2cid=8078245 |url=http://doc.rero.ch/record/289409/files/S1754942600000407.pdf }}</ref><ref>Title VII of the Civil Rights Act of 1964; Equal employment Opportunity Commission (www.eeoc.gov)</ref>

The [[Civil Rights Act of 1964]] and [[Civil Rights Act of 1991|1991]] (Title VII) were passed into law to prevent the discrimination of individuals due to race, color, religion, sex, or national origin. The Pregnancy Discrimination Act was added as an amendment and protects women if they are pregnant or have a pregnancy-related condition.<ref name="EEOC">Equal Employment Opportunity Commission (www.eeoc.gov)</ref>

The [[Age Discrimination in Employment Act]] of 1967 prohibits discriminatory practice directed against individuals who are 40 years of age and older. Although some states (e.g. New York) do have laws preventing the discrimination of individuals younger than 40, no federal law exists.<ref>{{cite web |title=New York State Human Rights Law (Executive Law, Article 15) |url=http://www.dhr.state.ny.us/doc/hrl.pdf |publisher=Dhr.state.ny.us |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20111004034551/http://www.dhr.state.ny.us/doc/hrl.pdf |archive-date=2011-10-04 }}</ref>

The [[Americans with Disabilities Act of 1990]] protects qualified individuals who currently have or in the past have had a physical or mental disability (current users of illegal drugs are not covered under this Act). A person is covered if he has a disability that substantially limits a major life activity, has a history of a disability, is regarded by others as being disabled, or has a physical or mental impairment that is not transitory (lasting or expected to last six months or less) and minor. In order to be covered under this Act, the individual must be qualified for the job. A qualified individual is "an individual with a disability who, with or without reasonable accommodation, can perform the essential functions of the employment position that such individual holds or desires."<ref name="disability">Americans with Disability Act; Equal Employment Opportunity Commission (www.eeoc.gov)</ref> Unless the disability poses an "undue hardship," reasonable accommodations must be made by the organization. "In general, an accommodation is any change in the work environment or in the way things are customarily done that enables an individual with a disability to enjoy equal employment opportunities."<ref name="disability" /> Examples of reasonable accommodations are changing the workspace of an individual in a wheelchair to make it more wheelchair accessible, modifying work schedules, and/or modifying equipment.<ref>{{cite journal |last1=DeLeire |first1=Thomas |title=The Wage and Employment Effects of the Americans with Disabilities Act |url=https://archive.org/details/sim_journal-of-human-resources_fall-2000_35_4/page/693 |journal=The Journal of Human Resources |date=Autumn 2000 |volume=35 |issue=4 |pages=693–715 |doi=10.2307/146368 |jstor=146368 }}</ref> Employees are responsible for asking for accommodations to be made by their employer.<ref name="EEOC"/>

The most recent law to be passed is Title II of the [[Genetic Information Nondiscrimination Act]] of 2008. In essence, this law prohibits the discrimination of employees or applicants due to an individual's genetic information and family medical history information.

In rare circumstances, it is lawful for employers to base hiring decisions on protected class information if it is considered a [[Bona Fide Occupational Qualification]], that is, if it is a "qualification reasonably necessary to the normal operation of the particular business." For example, a movie studio may base a hiring decision on age if the actor they are hiring will play a youthful character in a film.<ref>Arvey, R. D., & Faley, R. H. (1988).  Fairness in Selecting Employees.  Reading, Massachusetts:  Addison-Wesley Publishing Company.{{page needed|date=September 2020}}</ref>

Given these laws, organizations are limited in the types of questions they legally are allowed to ask applicants in a job interview. Asking these questions may cause discrimination against protected classes, unless the information is considered a Bona Fide Occupational Qualification. For example, in the majority of situations it is illegal to ask in the USA (and some other countries) the following questions in an interview as a condition of employment:
	
* What is your date of birth?<ref name="legal1">{{cite journal |last1=Saks |first1=Alan M. |last2=McCarthy |first2=Julie M. |title=Effects of discriminatory interview questions and gender on applicant reactions |journal=Journal of Business and Psychology |date=4 November 2006 |volume=21 |issue=2 |pages=175–191 |doi=10.1007/s10869-006-9024-7 |s2cid=144021364 }}</ref>
* Have you ever been arrested for a crime?<ref name="legal1" />
* Do you have any future plans for marriage and children?<ref name="legal1" />
* What are your spiritual beliefs?<ref name="legal2">US Equal Employment Opportunity Commission (www.eeoc.gov)</ref>
* How many days were you sick last year? Have you ever been treated for mental health problems?<ref name="legal2" />
* What prescription drugs are you currently taking?<ref name="legal2" />

=== Applicants with disabilities ===
Applicants with disabilities may be concerned with the effect that their disability has on both interview and employment outcomes. Research has concentrated on four key issues: how interviewers rate applicants with disabilities, the reactions of applicants with disabilities to the interview, the effects of disclosing a disability during the interview, and the perceptions different kinds of applicant disabilities may have on interviewer ratings.

The job interview is a tool used to measure constructs or overall characteristics that are relevant for the job. Oftentimes, applicants will receive a score based on their performance during the interview. Research has found different findings based on interviewers' perceptions of the disability. For example, some research has found a leniency effect (i.e., applicants with disabilities receive higher ratings than equally qualified non-disabled applicants) in ratings of applicants with disabilities<ref>{{cite journal |last1=Brecher |first1=Ellyn |last2=Bragger |first2=Jennifer |last3=Kutcher |first3=Eugene |title=The Structured Interview: Reducing Biases Toward Job Applicants with Physical Disabilities |journal=Employee Responsibilities and Rights Journal |date=28 November 2006 |volume=18 |issue=3 |pages=155–170 |doi=10.1007/s10672-006-9014-y |s2cid=144231940 }}</ref><ref name="Nordstrom">{{cite journal |last1=Nordstrom |first1=Cynthia R. |last2=Huffaker |first2=Bill J. |last3=Williams |first3=Karen B. |title=When Physical Disabilities Are Not Liabilities: The Role of Applicant and Interviewer Characteristics on Employment Interview Outcomes |journal=Journal of Applied Social Psychology |date=February 1998 |volume=28 |issue=4 |pages=283–306 |doi=10.1111/j.1559-1816.1998.tb01707.x }}</ref> Other research, however, has found there is a disconnect between the interview score and the hiring recommendation for applicants with disabilities. That is, even though applicants with disabilities may have received a high interview score, they are still not recommended for employment.<ref>{{cite journal |last1=Macan |first1=Therese Hoff |last2=Hayes |first2=Theodore L. |title=Both sides of the employment interview interaction: Perceptions of interviewers and applicants with disabilities. |journal=Rehabilitation Psychology |date=1995 |volume=40 |issue=4 |pages=261–278 |doi=10.1037/0090-5550.40.4.261 }}</ref><ref name="Miceli, N. S. 2002">{{cite journal |last1=Miceli |first1=Nicholas S. |last2=Harvey |first2=Michael |last3=Buckley |first3=M. Ronald |title=Potential discrimination in structured employment interviews |url=https://archive.org/details/sim_employee-responsibilities-and-rights-journal_2001-03_13_1/page/15 |journal=Employee Responsibilities and Rights Journal |date=2001 |volume=13 |issue=1 |pages=15–38 |doi=10.1023/a:1014430107659 |s2cid=142934650 }}</ref> The difference between ratings and hiring could be detrimental to a company because they may be missing an opportunity to hire a qualified applicant.

A second issue in interview research deals with the applicants' with disabilities reactions to the interview and applicant perceptions of the interviewers. Applicants with disabilities and able-bodied applicants report similar feelings of anxiety towards an interview.<ref name="Duckett">{{cite journal |last1=Duckett |first1=Paul S. |title=Disabling Employment Interviews: Warfare to work |journal=Disability & Society |date=December 2000 |volume=15 |issue=7 |pages=1019–1039 |doi=10.1080/713662022 |s2cid=145084174 }}</ref> Applicants with disabilities often report that interviewers react nervously and insecurely, which leads such applicants to experience anxiety and tension themselves. The interview is felt to be the part of the selection process where covert discrimination against applicants with disabilities can occur.<ref name="Duckett"/> Many applicants with disabilities feel they cannot disclose (i.e., inform potential employer of disability) or discuss their disability because they want to demonstrate their abilities. If the disability is visible, then disclosure will inevitably occur when the applicant meets the interviewer, so the applicant can decide if they want to discuss their disability. If an applicant has a non-visible disability, however, then that applicant has more of a choice in disclosing and discussing. In addition, applicants who were aware that the recruiting employer already had employed people with disabilities felt they had a more positive interview experience.<ref name="Duckett"/> Applicants should consider if they are comfortable with talking about and answering questions about their disability before deciding how to approach the interview.

Research has also demonstrated that different types of disabilities have different effects on interview outcomes. Disabilities with a negative stigma and that are perceived as resulting from the actions of the person (e.g., HIV-Positive, substance abuse) result in lower interview scores than disabilities for which the causes are perceived to be out of the individual's control (e.g., physical birth defect).<ref name="Miceli, N. S. 2002"/> A physical disability often results in higher interviewer ratings than psychological (e.g., mental illness) or sensory conditions (e.g., Tourette Syndrome).<ref name="Nordstrom"/><ref name="Dalgin">{{cite journal |last1=Spirito Dalgin |first1=Rebecca |last2=Bellini |first2=James |title=Invisible Disability Disclosure in an Employment Interview: Impact on Employers' Hiring Decisions and Views of Employability |url=https://archive.org/details/sim_rehabilitation-counseling-bulletin_2008-10_52_1/page/6 |journal=Rehabilitation Counseling Bulletin |date=October 2008 |volume=52 |issue=1 |pages=6–15 |doi=10.1177/0034355207311311 |s2cid=145125268 }}</ref> In addition, there are differences between the effects of disclosing disabilities that are visible (e.g., using a wheelchair) and non-visible (e.g., Epilepsy) during the interview. When applicants had a non-visible disability and disclosed their disability early in the interview they were not rated more negatively than applicants who did not disclose. In fact, they were liked more than the applicants who did not disclose their disability and were presumed not disabled.<ref>{{cite journal |last1=Roberts |first1=Lisa L. |last2=Macan |first2=Therese Hoff |title=Disability Disclosure Effects on Employment Interview Ratings of Applicants With Nonvisible Disabilities. |journal=Rehabilitation Psychology |date=2006 |volume=51 |issue=3 |pages=239–246 |doi=10.1037/0090-5550.51.3.239 }}</ref> Interviewers tend to be impressed by the honesty of the disclosure.<ref name="Dalgin"/> Strong caution needs to be taken with applying results from studies about specific disabilities, as these results may not apply to other types of disabilities. Not all disabilities are the same and more research is needed to find whether these results are relevant for other types of disabilities.

Some practical implications for job interviews for applicants with disabilities include research findings that show there are no differences in interviewer responses to a brief, shorter discussion or a detailed, longer discussion about the disability during the interview.<ref name="Dalgin"/> Applicants, however, should note that when a non-visible disability is disclosed near the end of the interview, applicants were rated more negatively than early disclosing and non-disclosing applicants. Therefore, it is possible that interviewers feel individuals who delay disclosure may do so out of shame or embarrassment.<ref>{{cite web |last1=Milani |first1=Silvia |title=Embarrassing job interview |url=https://relationshiplatestnews.wordpress.com/2018/03/23/the-most-embarrassing-job-interviews |work=Relationship Tips and Advice |date=2018-03-23 }}</ref>{{self-published inline|date=September 2020}} In addition, if the disability is disclosed after being hired, employers may feel deceived by the new hire and reactions could be less positive than would have been in the interview.<ref>{{cite journal |last1=Stone |first1=Dianna L. |last2=Colella |first2=Adrienne |title=A Model of Factors Affecting the Treatment of Disabled Individuals in Organizations |url=https://archive.org/details/sim_academy-of-management-review_1996-04_21_2/page/352 |journal=Academy of Management Review |date=April 1996 |volume=21 |issue=2 |pages=352–401 |doi=10.5465/amr.1996.9605060216 }}</ref> If applicants want to disclose their disability during the interview, research shows that a disclosure and/or discussion earlier in the interview approach may afford them some positive interview effects.<ref>{{cite journal |last1=Hebl |first1=Michelle R. |last2=Skorinko |first2=Jeanine L. |title=Acknowledging One's Physical Disability in the Interview: Does "When" Make a Difference? |journal=Journal of Applied Social Psychology |date=December 2005 |volume=35 |issue=12 |pages=2477–2492 |doi=10.1111/j.1559-1816.2005.tb02111.x }}</ref> The positive effects, however, are preceded by the interviewers perception of the applicants' psychological well-being. That is, when the interviewer perceives the applicant is psychologically well and/or comfortable with his or her disability, there can be positive interviewer effects. In contrast, if the interviewer perceives the applicant as uncomfortable or anxious discussing the disability, this may either fail to garner positive effect or result in more negative interview ratings for the candidate. Caution must again be taken when applying these research findings to other types of disabilities not investigated in the studies discussed above. There are many factors that can influence the interview of an applicant with a disability, such as whether the disability is physical or psychological, visible or non-visible, or whether the applicant is perceived as responsible for the disability or not. Therefore, applicants should make their own conclusions about how to proceed in the interview after comparing their situations with those examined in the research discussed here.{{citation needed|date=September 2020}}

=== Applicants with criminal backgrounds ===

Although it is illegal{{Where|date=November 2018}} for employers to ask about applicants' arrest record during an interview as a deciding factor in applicant hiring decisions, employers do have the right to obtain information about applicants' criminal convictions before hiring, including during the interview phase.<ref name="legal1"/> Many companies consider hiring applicants with criminal history a liability. For instance, if a company hired someone with an assault charge and that person later assaulted another employee or vendor, some people would say that the company was liable or legally responsible for not maintaining a safe work environment. Although the legalities are more complex, this potential responsibility an organization may carry often is a reason why many companies conduct criminal background checks. When making hiring decisions that somewhat depend on one's criminal background, employers must consider the following:

* Employers should only ask about an applicant's criminal conviction history if it is job related.<ref name="eeoc.gov">{{Cite web |url=https://www.eeoc.gov/laws/practices/inquiries_arrest_conviction.cfm |title=Pre-Employment Inquiries and Arrest & Conviction |access-date=2017-04-05 |archive-date=2020-04-29 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20200429212220/https://www.eeoc.gov/laws/practices/inquiries_arrest_conviction.cfm }}</ref>
* Treating job applicants with criminal histories differently based on their race or national origin is a disparate treatment liability.<ref name="ReferenceB">{{Cite web|url=https://www.eeoc.gov/laws/guidance/arrest_conviction.cfm|title = Enforcement Guidance on the Consideration of Arrest and Conviction Records in Employment Decisions under Title VII of the Civil Rights Act &#124; U.S. Equal Employment Opportunity Commission| date=25 April 2012 }}</ref> Disparate treatment is defined as intentional discrimination<ref>{{cite book |last1=Landy |first1=Frank J. |year=2005 |chapter=Disparate Treatment |pages=204–205 |chapter-url=https://books.google.com/books?id=M8KwBAaHF6AC&pg=PA204 |title=Employment Discrimination Litigation: Behavioral, Quantitative, and Legal Perspectives |publisher=John Wiley & Sons |isbn=978-0-7879-7819-8 }}</ref> If employers ask about criminal convictions in the interview process, the interviewer must ask all interviewees and not just interviewees of a perceived sex, race, or national origin. 
* Excluding applicants with certain criminal records may end up overly excluding groups of individuals protected under Title VII<ref>{{Cite web |url=https://www.eeoc.gov/laws/statutes/titlevii.cfm |title=Title VII of the Civil Rights Act of 1964}}</ref> which is a disparate impact liability.<ref name="ReferenceB"/> Disparate impact is defined as unintentional discrimination.<ref>{{cite book |last1=Landy |first1=Frank J. |year=2005 |chapter=Disparate Impact |pages=205–206 |chapter-url=https://books.google.com/books?id=M8KwBAaHF6AC&pg=PA205 |title=Employment Discrimination Litigation: Behavioral, Quantitative, and Legal Perspectives |publisher=John Wiley & Sons |isbn=978-0-7879-7819-8 }}</ref> 
* Some states have different laws about how arrest and conviction records can be used in hiring decisions and when employers can obtain information about criminal records.<ref name="eeoc.gov"/>

Although not much research has been conducted to examine whether applicants should talk about their criminal histories or not, a 2012 study<ref name="Swanson, S. J. 2012">{{cite journal |last1=Swanson |first1=Sarah J. |last2=Langfitt-Reese |first2=Sandra |last3=Bond |first3=Gary R. |title=Employer attitudes about criminal histories. |journal=Psychiatric Rehabilitation Journal |date=September 2012 |volume=35 |issue=5 |pages=385–390 |doi=10.1037/h0094498 |pmid=23116380 }}</ref> found that employers were more likely to hire someone with a criminal record if the applicant made face-to-face contact with the employer and was prepared and willing to discuss their job related knowledge. Applicants also had an increased chance of being hired if they discussed what they learned from their experience in the justice system, as well as how they were rehabilitated, during the interview. This study found that employers preferred applicants that revealed their criminal records upfront and were willing to take responsibility for their actions.<ref name="Swanson, S. J. 2012"/>

[[Ban the Box]] is a campaign to remove the question about criminal history from job applications as an opportunity to give people with criminal histories a reasonable chance in the employment selection process. By allowing applicants to be interviewed before disclosing their criminal histories, this campaign seeks to increase the number of applicants with criminal histories in the workplace.<ref>{{cite journal |last1=Kuhn |first1=Kristine M. |title=What We Overlook: Background Checks and Their Implications for Discrimination |journal=Industrial and Organizational Psychology |date=December 2013 |volume=6 |issue=4 |pages=419–423 |doi=10.1111/iops.12077 |s2cid=145228652 |url=https://zenodo.org/record/895376 }}</ref> The campaign focuses on how discrimination in the recruiting phase of selection makes it harder for people with criminal convictions to obtain employment. Not having employment makes it harder for people with criminal histories to support their families, and a lack of a job can lead to an increased chance of the person becoming a repeat offender.<ref>{{cite news |id={{ProQuest|815893690}} |last1=Mayse |first1=James |title=Felony conviction a hurdle to getting hired |work=McClatchy - Tribune Business News |date=5 December 2010 }}</ref>

=== Discrimination due to weight or pregnancy ===

Job applicants who are underweight (to the point of emaciation), overweight or obese may face discrimination in the interview.<ref name="Roehling">{{cite journal |last1=Roehling |first1=Mark V. |title=Weight-Based Discrimination in Employment: Psychological and Legal Aspects |journal=Personnel Psychology |date=December 1999 |volume=52 |issue=4 |pages=969–1016 |doi=10.1111/j.1744-6570.1999.tb00186.x }}</ref><ref name="Swami">{{cite journal |last1=Swami |first1=Viren |last2=Chan |first2=Flora |last3=Wong |first3=Vivien |last4=Furnham |first4=Adrian |last5=Tovée |first5=Martin J. |title=Weight-Based Discrimination in Occupational Hiring and Helping Behavior |journal=Journal of Applied Social Psychology |date=April 2008 |volume=38 |issue=4 |pages=968–981 |doi=10.1111/j.1559-1816.2008.00334.x }}</ref> The negative treatment of overweight and obese individuals may stem from beliefs that weight is controllable and those who fail to control their weight are lazy, unmotivated, and lack self-discipline.<ref name="Greenleaf">{{cite journal |last1=Greenleaf |first1=Christy |last2=Starks |first2=Misty |last3=Gomez |first3=Laura |last4=Chambliss |first4=Heather |last5=Martin |first5=Scott |title=Weight-related words associated with figure silhouettes |journal=Body Image |date=December 2004 |volume=1 |issue=4 |pages=373–384 |doi=10.1016/j.bodyim.2004.10.004 |pmid=18089167 }}</ref><ref name="Bellizzi">{{cite journal |last1=Bellizzi |first1=Joseph A. |last2=Hasty |first2=Ronald W. |title=Territory Assignment Decisions and Supervising Unethical Selling Behavior: The Effects of Obesity and Gender as Moderated by Job-Related Factors |journal=Journal of Personal Selling & Sales Management |date=1 January 1998 |volume=18 |issue=2 |pages=35–49 |doi=10.1080/08853134.1998.10754129 |id={{ProQuest|216749832}} |jstor=40471680 |s2cid=151726125 }}</ref> Underweight individuals may also be subject to appearance-related negative treatment.<ref name="Swami"/> Underweight, overweight and obese applicants are not protected from discrimination by any current United States laws.<ref name="Roehling"/> However, some individuals who are morbidly obese and whose obesity is due to a physiological disorder may be protected against discrimination under the Americans with Disabilities Act.<ref name="King">{{cite journal |last1=King |first1=Eden B. |last2=Shapiro |first2=Jenessa R. |last3=Hebl |first3=Michelle R. |last4=Singletary |first4=Sarah L. |last5=Turner |first5=Stacey |title=The stigma of obesity in customer service: A mechanism for remediation and bottom-line consequences of interpersonal discrimination. |url=https://archive.org/details/sim_journal-of-applied-psychology_2006-05_91_3/page/579 |journal=Journal of Applied Psychology |date=2006 |volume=91 |issue=3 |pages=579–593 |doi=10.1037/0021-9010.91.3.579 |pmid=16737356 }}</ref>

Discrimination against pregnant applicants is illegal under the Pregnancy Discrimination Act of 1978, which views pregnancy as a temporary disability and requires employers to treat pregnant applicants the same as all other applicants.<ref name="US">{{cite web |title=U.S. Equal Employment Opportunity Commission (2011) Pregnancy discrimination |url=https://www.eeoc.gov/laws/types/pregnancy.cfm |publisher=Eeoc.gov }}</ref> Yet, discrimination against pregnant applicants continues both in the United States and internationally.<ref name="US"/><ref name="Gatrell">{{cite journal |last1=Gatrell |first1=Caroline |title=Managing the Maternal Body: A Comprehensive Review and Transdisciplinary Analysis: Managing the Maternal Body |journal=International Journal of Management Reviews |date=March 2011 |volume=13 |issue=1 |pages=97–112 |doi=10.1111/j.1468-2370.2010.00286.x |s2cid=142958889 |doi-access=free }}</ref> Research shows that pregnant applicants compared to non-pregnant applicants are less likely to be recommended for hire.<ref name="Bragger">{{cite journal |last1=Bragger |first1=Jennifer DeNicolis |last2=Kutcher |first2=Eugene |last3=Morgan |first3=John |last4=Firth |first4=Patricia |title=The Effects of the Structured Interview on Reducing Biases Against Pregnant Job Applicants |journal=Sex Roles |date=2002 |volume=46 |issue=7/8 |pages=215–226 |doi=10.1023/A:1019967231059 |s2cid=141006365 }}</ref><ref name="Cunningham">{{cite journal |last1=Cunningham |first1=Jennifer |last2=Macan |first2=Therese |title=Effects of Applicant Pregnancy on Hiring Decisions and Interview Ratings |journal=Sex Roles |date=19 September 2007 |volume=57 |issue=7–8 |pages=497–508 |doi=10.1007/s11199-007-9279-0 |s2cid=145686325 }}</ref> Interviewers appear concerned that pregnant applicants are more likely than non-pregnant applicants to miss work and even quit.<ref name="Cunningham"/> Organizations who wish to reduce potential discrimination against pregnant applicants should consider implementing structured interviews, although some theoretical work suggests interviewers may still show biases even in these types of interviews.<ref name="Bragger"/><ref>{{cite book |doi=10.1002/9781119992592.ch8 |chapter=Actions Speak Too: Uncovering Possible Implicit and Explicit Discrimination in the Employment Interview Process |title=International Review of Industrial and Organizational Psychology 2011 |year=2011 |last1=Macan |first1=Therese |last2=Merritt |first2=Stephanie |pages=293–337 |isbn=978-1-119-99259-2 }}</ref>

=== Other forms of discrimination ===

Employers are using social networking sites like Facebook and LinkedIn to obtain additional information about job applicants.<ref name="Cross">{{cite web |title=Cross-Tab (2010). Online reputation in a connected world |url=http://www.microsoft.com/privacy/dpd/research.aspx |publisher=Microsoft.com |archive-url=https://archive.today/20100411190004/http://www.microsoft.com/privacy/dpd/research.aspx |archive-date=April 11, 2010 |access-date=May 9, 2011 |url-status=live }}</ref><ref name="JobVite">{{cite web |publisher=[[Jobvite]]|year=2010 |title=Jobvite: 2010 social recruiting survey results |url=https://recruiting.jobvite.com/resources/social-recruiting-survey.php }}</ref><ref name="SHRM">{{cite book |year=2007 |title=2007 Advances in E-recruiting: Leveraging the .jobs Domain |publisher=Society for Human Resource Management |url=https://search.alexanderstreet.com/preview/work/bibliographic_entity%7Cbibliographic_details%7C2413344 |oclc=891140235 }}</ref> While these sites may be useful to verify resume information, profiles with pictures also may reveal much more information about the applicant, including issues pertaining to applicant weight and pregnancy.<ref name="Grasz">{{cite press release |title=Forty-five Percent of Employers Use Social Networking Sites to Research Job Candidates, CareerBuilder Survey Finds |publisher=CareerBuilder |date=19 August 2009 |url=http://press.careerbuilder.com/2009-08-19-Forty-five-Percent-of-Employers-Use-Social-Networking-Sites-to-Research-Job-Candidates-CareerBuilder-Survey-Finds |access-date=September 1, 2020 }}</ref> Some employers are also asking potential job candidates for their social media logins which has alarmed many privacy watch dogs and regulators.<ref name="Fox News">{{cite news |title=Senators call for federal probe over employers asking for Facebook passwords |url=https://www.foxnews.com/politics/senators-call-for-federal-probe-over-employers-asking-for-facebook-passwords/ |work=Fox News |agency=Associated Press |date=26 March 2015 }}</ref>

Although this article does discuss some issues of job applicant discrimination, there could be many more types and factors of discrimination in the workplace than noted here. The most common types of discrimination within the workplace are ethnic and gender/sexual orientation discrimination. In an experiment performed in the US by Marianne Bertrand and Sendhil Mullainathan, it was noted that job applicants "with white-sounding names got 50 percent more callbacks for interviews than those with African-American-sounding names"<ref>{{Citation|title=Employment discrimination|date=2020-11-17|url=https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Employment_discrimination&oldid=989214590|work=Wikipedia|language=en|access-date=2020-11-27}}</ref> This shows that something as simple as a person's name could be the reason they do or do not get a chance to have a job interview.

<!-- Deleted image removed: [[File:Frontiers Identifying Moderators in the Link Between Workplace Discrimination and Health-Well-Being.png|thumb]] -->
Another note to add to this article, is the idea of how discrimination within a workplace/job interview can affect a person's health and wellbeing. A person that is looking to find a job, no matter the industry, should not have to worry about whether or not they are a good candidate because of what discrimination might be placed against them. "Perceived discrimination can cause a lot of stress to an individual"<ref>{{Cite journal|last1=Xu|first1=Yue Ethel|last2=Chopik|first2=William J.|date=2020|title=Identifying Moderators in the Link Between Workplace Discrimination and Health/Well-Being|journal=Frontiers in Psychology|language=en|volume=11|article-number=458|doi=10.3389/fpsyg.2020.00458|pmid=32256433|pmc=7092632|issn=1664-1078|doi-access=free}}</ref> which in turn could make it more difficult for a person to get job/job interview.

==Cross-cultural issues==
As with the common comparisons between Eastern and Western cultures, interviews and the [[Construct (philosophy)|constructs]] assessed by the interview have been found to differ across the world. For example, studies of the United States of America (USA) to Canada have found conflicting results in average levels of agreeableness in each country.<ref name=":0">{{cite journal |last1=Heine |first1=Steven J. |last2=Buchtel |first2=Emma E. |last3=Norenzayan |first3=Ara |title=What Do Cross-National Comparisons of Personality Traits Tell Us?: The Case of Conscientiousness |journal=Psychological Science |date=April 2008 |volume=19 |issue=4 |pages=309–313 |doi=10.1111/j.1467-9280.2008.02085.x |pmid=18399880 |s2cid=3828183 }}</ref> People tend to use social comparison when reporting their own level of agreeableness.<ref name=":0" /> Even though Canadians are likely to be more agreeable, they might score similarly to those individuals from the USA.<ref name=":0" /> In situations where social comparison is a factor, an honest answer could result in under- or over-estimation.

Because of these cultural differences, more businesses are adding cross-cultural training to their HR training.<ref name=":1">{{cite journal |last1=Lievens |first1=Filip |last2=Harris |first2=Michael M. |last3=Van Keer |first3=Etienne |last4=Bisqueret |first4=Claire |title=Predicting cross-cultural training performance: The validity of personality, cognitive ability, and dimensions measured by an assessment center and a behavior description interview. |journal=Journal of Applied Psychology |date=2003 |volume=88 |issue=3 |pages=476–489 |doi=10.1037/0021-9010.88.3.476 |pmid=12814296 |s2cid=37456091 |url=https://ink.library.smu.edu.sg/lkcsb_research/5657 }}</ref><ref>{{cite book |last1=Sinangil |first1=H.K. |last2=Ones |first2=D.S. |year=2001 |chapter=Expatriate management: Personnel psychology |editor1-last=Anderson |editor1-first=N. |editor2-last=Ones |editor2-first=D.S. |editor3-last=Sinangil |editor3-first=H.K. |editor4-last=Viswesvaran |editor4-first=C. |title=Handbook of Industrial, Work and Organizational Psychology |url=https://archive.org/details/handbookindustri02ande |publisher=Sage |pages=[https://archive.org/details/handbookindustri02ande/page/n452 424]–443 }}</ref> The goal of cross-cultural training is to improve one's ability to adapt and judge people from other cultures. This training is a first step in ensuring the process of using the job interview to decide whom to hire works the same in a selection situation where there are cross-cultural factors.

One cultural difference in the job interview is in the type of questions applicants will expect and not expect to be asked.<ref name=":2">{{cite journal |last1=Posthuma |first1=Richard A. |last2=Levashina |first2=Julia |last3=Lievens |first3=Filip |last4=Schollaert |first4=Eveline |last5=Tsai |first5=Wei-Chi |last6=Wagstaff |first6=Maria Fernanda |last7=Campion |first7=Michael A. |title=Comparing employment interviews in Latin America with other countries |url=https://archive.org/details/sim_journal-of-business-research_2014-05_67_5/page/943 |journal=Journal of Business Research |date=May 2014 |volume=67 |issue=5 |pages=943–951 |doi=10.1016/j.jbusres.2013.07.014 |s2cid=8935071 }}</ref> Interviewers outside the USA often ask about family, marital status, and children.<ref name=":2" /> These types of questions are usually not allowed by USA job laws but are acceptable in other countries. Applicants can be surprised by questions interviewers ask them that are not appropriate or consistent with their own cultures. For example, in Belgium and Russia, interviewers are unlikely to ask about an applicant's personal values, opinions and beliefs.<ref name=":2" /> Thus, USA interviewers who do ask applicants about their values can make non-USA applicants uneasy or misinterpret the reason they are not prepared.

Another difference is in the consistency with which common constructs, even those that generalize across cultures, predict across different countries and cultures. For example, those who seem high in [[Big Five personality traits#Agreeableness|Agreeableness]] can do less well on the job in European workplaces.<ref name=":1" /> But those high in Agreeableness in the USA or Japan will do better on the job as measured on the same criteria.<ref name=":1" /> In some cases the structured Behavior Description Interview (BDI) that predicts who will do well on the job in some countries, from their interview scores, fails to predict accurately which applicants to hire in other countries.<ref name=":1" />

===Methodological biases===
====Construct bias====
There are a few ways that cross-cultural differences can mess up the results of our attempts to predict job performance.<ref name=":3">{{cite journal |last1=van de Vijver |first1=Fons J. R. |last2=Poortinga |first2=Ype H. |title=Towards an Integrated Analysis of Bias in Cross-Cultural Assessment |journal=European Journal of Psychological Assessment |date=January 1997 |volume=13 |issue=1 |pages=29–37 |doi=10.1027/1015-5759.13.1.29 |url=https://pure.uvt.nl/ws/files/222002/26699_11820.pdf }}</ref> The first source of error is construct bias, the possibility that the construct being measured is viewed differently by those from another culture if it exists at all. One way this could happen is if the behaviors a person displays, that go with that construct, are viewed differently in different cultures. It could also be the extent to which the construct even exists in their country. For example, the Multidimensional Work Ethic Profile (MWEP), is a scale demonstrated to work across many countries.<ref>{{cite journal |last1=Lim |first1=Doo H. |last2=Woehr |first2=David J. |last3=You |first3=Yeong Mahn |last4=Allen Gorman |first4=C. |title=The translation and development of a short form of the Korean language version of the multidimensional work ethic profile |journal=Human Resource Development International |date=September 2007 |volume=10 |issue=3 |pages=319–331 |doi=10.1080/13678860701515406 |s2cid=44203819 }}</ref><ref>{{cite journal |last1=Woehr |first1=David J. |last2=Arciniega |first2=Luis M. |last3=Lim |first3=Doo H. |title=Examining Work Ethic Across Populations: A Comparison of the Multidimensional Work Ethic Profile Across Three Diverse Cultures |url=https://archive.org/details/sim_educational-and-psychological-measurement_2007-02_67_1/page/154 |journal=Educational and Psychological Measurement |date=February 2007 |volume=67 |issue=1 |pages=154–168 |doi=10.1177/0013164406292036 |s2cid=143045918 }}</ref><ref>{{cite journal |last1=Yousef |first1=Darwish A. |title=Islamic work ethic – A moderator between organizational commitment and job satisfaction in a cross-cultural context |journal=Personnel Review |date=April 2001 |volume=30 |issue=2 |pages=152–169 |doi=10.1108/00483480110380325 }}</ref> However, in China the MWEP concept/dimension of Leisure has been shown to have poor equivalence with other countries, and may be a culturally inappropriate assessment due to the Confucian concept of hard work without leisure.<ref>{{cite thesis |last1=Chen |first1=Danxia |title=Eastern Work Ethic: Structural Validity, Measurement Invariance, and Generational Differences |url=https://digital.library.unt.edu/ark:/67531/metadc500082/ }}</ref> Research has shown that differences in the levels of established cross-cultural constructs such as [[Michele J. Gelfand#Cultural tightness-looseness|Cultural Tightness-Looseness]] increase or decrease the effect of the Five Factor Model personality traits.<ref>{{cite journal |last1=Shao |first1=Lian |last2=Webber |first2=Sheila |title=A cross-cultural test of the 'five-factor model of personality and transformational leadership' |journal=Journal of Business Research |date=August 2006 |volume=59 |issue=8 |pages=936–944 |doi=10.1016/j.jbusres.2006.02.005 |url=https://spectrum.library.concordia.ca/1557/1/MQ83965.pdf }}</ref> Tight cultures have strong social norms and adherence coupled with low tolerance for behavior that deviates from those norms, and loose cultures are the opposite with weak norms and high tolerance for deviance.<ref>{{cite journal |last1=Gelfand |first1=M. J. |last2=Raver |first2=J. L. |last3=Nishii |first3=L. |last4=Leslie |first4=L. M. |last5=Lun |first5=J. |last6=Lim |first6=B. C. |last7=Duan |first7=L. |last8=Almaliach |first8=A. |last9=Ang |first9=S. |last10=Arnadottir |first10=J. |last11=Aycan |first11=Z. |last12=Boehnke |first12=K. |last13=Boski |first13=P. |last14=Cabecinhas |first14=R. |last15=Chan |first15=D. |last16=Chhokar |first16=J. |last17=D'Amato |first17=A. |last18=Ferrer |first18=M. |last19=Fischlmayr |first19=I. C. |last20=Fischer |first20=R. |last21=Fulop |first21=M. |last22=Georgas |first22=J. |last23=Kashima |first23=E. S. |last24=Kashima |first24=Y. |last25=Kim |first25=K. |last26=Lempereur |first26=A. |last27=Marquez |first27=P. |last28=Othman |first28=R. |last29=Overlaet |first29=B. |last30=Panagiotopoulou |first30=P. |last31=Peltzer |first31=K. |last32=Perez-Florizno |first32=L. R. |last33=Ponomarenko |first33=L. |last34=Realo |first34=A. |last35=Schei |first35=V. |last36=Schmitt |first36=M. |last37=Smith |first37=P. B. |last38=Soomro |first38=N. |last39=Szabo |first39=E. |last40=Taveesin |first40=N. |last41=Toyama |first41=M. |last42=Van de Vliert |first42=E. |last43=Vohra |first43=N. |last44=Ward |first44=C. |last45=Yamaguchi |first45=S. |title=Differences Between Tight and Loose Cultures: A 33-Nation Study |journal=Science |date=27 May 2011 |volume=332 |issue=6033 |pages=1100–1104 |doi=10.1126/science.1197754 |pmid=21617077 |bibcode=2011Sci...332.1100G |hdl=1813/75456 |s2cid=18083852 |url=https://digitalcommons.ilr.cornell.edu/cgi/viewcontent.cgi?article=2302&context=articles |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20190427075824/https://digitalcommons.ilr.cornell.edu/cgi/viewcontent.cgi?article=2302&context=articles |url-status=dead |archive-date=April 27, 2019 |hdl-access=free }}</ref> An interviewer from a tight culture might view the normal behaviors of a loose cultured interviewee as signs of a poor moral character despite the behavior being normal. As such, differences between the tightness-looseness of the interviewer's and interviewee's home countries can introduce method bias, negatively affecting the interviewer's assessment of the interviewee's answers and behaviors. First construct bias must be measured by comparing groups of persons from distinct cultures and comparing if any real differences are discovered. The information on those differences can be used to make the adjustments needed to allow the construct to measure what it is intended to measure in people from different cultures.

====Method bias====
[[Response bias]] is another cross-cultural difference that has been shown to affect how we measure constructs and interpret the results.<ref>{{cite journal |last1=Fischer |first1=Ronald |title=Standardization to Account for Cross-Cultural Response Bias: A Classification of Score Adjustment Procedures and Review of Research in JCCP |url=https://archive.org/details/sim_journal-of-cross-cultural-psychology_2004-05_35_3/page/263 |journal=Journal of Cross-Cultural Psychology |date=May 2004 |volume=35 |issue=3 |pages=263–282 |doi=10.1177/0022022104264122 |s2cid=32046329 }}</ref> [[Social desirability bias]] is a tendency to give a socially acceptable answer, even if it is a lie because we want to look good. Giving socially acceptable, but partly or completely false, answers can inflate interview scores.<ref name=":4">{{cite journal |last1=Odendaal |first1=Aletta |title=Cross-cultural differences in social desirability scales: Influence of cognitive ability |journal=SA Journal of Industrial Psychology |date=5 February 2015 |volume=41 |issue=1 |page=13|doi=10.4102/sajip.v41i1.1259 |doi-access=free }}</ref> One simple example of socially acceptable answers is called acquiescence bias, which is a tendency to agree with all questions with a positive meaning.<ref name=":4" /> People also have been found to show different attitudes towards answers on the extreme high and low end of a set of options (extremely agree or extremely disagree).<ref name=":4" /> In some cases, people from different cultures may just be unfamiliar with a word (term, concept, context) or with a type of question.<ref name=":4" /> Another research study found that self and other reports of conscientiousness failed to relate with expected job behaviors across cultures, demonstrating that one of the most predictive constructs in the USA is tied to aspects of USA culture that may not be present in a different type of culture.<ref name=":0" />

For example, in the West, applicants prefer to eliminate details and focus on the larger issue, tending towards a comprehensive evaluation starting from individual elements and then moving towards the whole.<ref name=":5">{{cite book |last1=Lievens |first1=Filip |chapter=International situational judgment tests |pages=279–300 |chapter-url=https://ink.library.smu.edu.sg/lkcsb_research/5813 |editor1-last=Weekley |editor1-first=Jeff A. |editor2-last=Ployhart |editor2-first=Robert E. |year=2006 |title=Situational Judgment Tests: Theory, Measurement, and Application |publisher=Lawrence Erlbaum Associates |isbn=978-0-8058-5251-6 }}</ref> In Japan, a respondent would go from the general to the specific in answering, preferring to divide a problem and analyze it piece by piece. Likewise, there are differences between individualist and collectivist cultures in the types of answers they chose. When given a series of options, individualists tend to choose the task-oriented option that involves direct communication with others.<ref name=":5" /> Yet collectivists choose the option that sees group harmony and protecting or saving face for others as more important.<ref name=":5" /> These differences can introduce method bias when interviewers evaluate or score how the applicant did in the interview. This is why it is important to understand how and why the best answer in one culture is not the best elsewhere. It might even be completely wrong.

====Item bias====
There is also item bias introduced by the actual items or questions in an interview. Poor item translation can be a problem.<ref name=":3" /> This might be incorrectly translating the same item to another language such as in an organization that hires both English and Spanish-speaking employees. Or it might be in someone not understanding the wording of an item because they are not native to that country's language. Similar to construct bias, the wording of an item can result in measuring different traits because of different meanings in the two different cultures.
